# Abstract

Physical facilities, maintenance, and construction form the backbone of quality education delivery in Higher Education Institutions (HEIs). A well-structured infrastructure framework ensures not only the smooth functioning of academic and administrative activities but also contributes directly to student learning outcomes, research productivity, institutional reputation, and compliance with accreditation benchmarks. This book, *Physical Facilities, Maintenance, and Construction in Higher Education Institutions (HEIs): Policies, Registers, and Best Practices*, presents a comprehensive approach to managing campus infrastructure in alignment with national and international quality assurance frameworks.

The work emphasizes the holistic management of facilities including classrooms, laboratories, libraries, research centers, hostels, canteens, sports complexes, administrative blocks, and digital infrastructure such as ERP, LMS, and ICT services. It highlights the importance of maintenance policies—preventive, corrective, and annual contracts (AMCs)—in ensuring sustainability, safety, and longevity of resources. Special attention is given to compliance with NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE, AICTE, UGC, and ABET norms, which mandate systematic documentation, record-keeping, and audit-ready evidence to demonstrate effective utilization of infrastructure.

Registers and reporting mechanisms form a unique focus of the book. Templates for compliance registers, audit reports, facility allocation records, maintenance schedules, vendor agreements, and waste management systems are provided to support institutions in operationalizing policy frameworks. By standardizing documentation practices, the book enables institutions to integrate physical facilities governance with quality assurance and accreditation readiness.

Another key dimension addressed is sustainability and green campus development. Policies and registers for energy management, solar power, water conservation, waste recycling, and eco-friendly construction are mapped to Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 3, 6, 9, 11, 12, and 13)**,** ensuring that HEIs contribute to national and global sustainability agendas. The book also emphasizes safety and security through building codes, fire safety policies, disaster preparedness, and accessibility for differently-abled persons, thereby promoting inclusivity and resilience in infrastructure planning.

Through policy drafts, compliance checklists, registers, and model reports, this work equips administrators, estate officers, IQAC members, and academic leaders with practical tools for effective governance. It bridges the gap between theoretical policy frameworks and operational evidence required during institutional audits.

In conclusion, this book provides a practical, policy-driven, and accreditation-focused framework for managing physical facilities in HEIs. It is designed to serve as a handbook for institutional leaders, accreditation teams, and quality assurance professionals, ensuring that infrastructure development and maintenance are strategically aligned with educational excellence, regulatory compliance, and sustainable growth.

# 1. Physical Facilities, Maintenance and Construction in Higher Education Institutions (HEIs)

## 1. Introduction

- Physical infrastructure is a fundamental enabler of quality education in HEIs.

- It encompasses academic facilities (classrooms, labs, libraries), residential facilities (hostels, staff quarters), administrative spaces, sports, healthcare, and utility infrastructure.

- Well-planned physical facilities combined with systematic maintenance and construction policies ensure compliance with NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE, and NEP-2020 benchmarks.

## 2. Physical Facilities in HEIs

### a) Academic Facilities

- **Classrooms:** Equipped with ICT (projectors, smart boards, Wi-Fi).

- **Laboratories:** Subject-specific labs (science, engineering, language, computer).

- **Libraries:** Digital + physical collections, reading halls, e-resource centers.

- **Research Centers:** Dedicated spaces for innovation, incubation, and high-performance computing.

- **Examination Halls:** Secured spaces with CCTV, biometric access, and ICT support.

### b) Administrative Facilities

- Offices for management, departments, examination cell, IQAC, and finance.

- ERP-enabled record rooms and data centers.

- Meeting halls, seminar halls, and conference rooms.

### c) Student Facilities

- Hostels with study spaces, Wi-Fi, and common areas.

- Canteens, health centers, and counseling rooms.

- Sports facilities (indoor & outdoor), gyms, auditoriums.

### d) Utility & Support Infrastructure

- Power backup systems (UPS, generators, solar panels).

- Safe drinking water and sanitation facilities.

- Campus transport and parking areas.

- Green initiatives – rainwater harvesting, waste management, e-waste recycling.

## 3. Maintenance of Physical Facilities

### a) Preventive Maintenance

- Scheduled inspections of electrical systems, air conditioning, lifts, fire safety.

- Regular servicing of ICT equipment (projectors, UPS, lab systems).

- Landscaping and campus cleanliness through facility management teams.

### b) Corrective Maintenance

- Repair/replacement of faulty equipment and furniture.

- Emergency maintenance for network outages, water leakages, or power failures.

- AMC (Annual Maintenance Contracts) for ICT, lab equipment, and utilities.

### c) Maintenance Registers

- Hardware/Software Maintenance Register (IT Cell).

- Building Maintenance Logbook (Engineering/Works Department).

- Lab & Equipment Calibration Records (Science/Engineering Departments).

- Obsolete Equipment Register (IT + Stores).

## 4. Construction in HEIs

### a) Planning and Development

- Construction aligned with the Master Plan of the institution.

- New buildings designed as eco-friendly, energy-efficient, and disability-inclusive.

- Proposals vetted by Planning & Development Committee and approved by Governing Body.

### b) Funding & Approvals

- Internal budget allocations.

- Grants from UGC, AICTE, RUSA, World Bank Projects (TEQIP, EQUIP).

- CSR contributions or PPP (Public-Private Partnership) initiatives.

### c) Execution & Monitoring

- Engineering/Works Department executes projects with transparent tendering process.

- Use of modern construction technology (prefab, green buildings).

- Monthly progress reports submitted to Planning Committee.

### d) Safety & Compliance

- Compliance with local building codes, fire safety, environmental laws, and accessibility standards.

- Audit by internal and external committees at each stage.

## 5. Governance and Accountability

- Engineering/Works Department → Oversees construction and building maintenance.

- IT Cell → Maintains ICT-related infrastructure.

- Library Committee → Oversees digital and physical library facilities.

- Hostel & Student Affairs Committee → Maintains hostels, canteens, and student services.

- Registrar/Finance Officer → Approves budgets, monitors expenditure.

## 6. Accreditation and Quality Assurance Linkages

- **NAAC:** Criterion 4 (Infrastructure and Learning Resources) directly assesses adequacy and maintenance of facilities.

- **NBA:** Criterion 7 (Facilities and Technical Support) emphasizes well-maintained labs and classrooms.

- **NIRF:** TLR (Teaching, Learning & Resources) considers infrastructure as a core parameter.

- **QS I·GAUGE:** Facilities, Diversity & Accessibility indicators include physical and ICT resources.

- **SDGs:** SDG 9 (Infrastructure), SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities & Communities), SDG 12 (Responsible Consumption).

## 7. Challenges in Physical Facilities & Maintenance

- Budget constraints for modernization.

- Delays in construction due to administrative bottlenecks.

- Rising maintenance costs for ICT and lab equipment.

- E-waste and hazardous waste management.

- Balancing heritage buildings with modern infrastructure requirements.

## 8. Recommendations

- Adopt Facility Management Software (FMS) for monitoring repairs and maintenance.

- Implement Green Building Codes and renewable energy systems.

- Strengthen PPP models for infrastructure funding.

- Schedule quarterly maintenance audits.

- Introduce feedback mechanisms for users (faculty, staff, students).

## 9. Conclusion

Physical facilities are the backbone of quality higher education delivery. When combined with structured maintenance protocols and sustainable construction practices, they ensure that institutions remain future-ready, accreditation-compliant, and supportive of academic excellence, innovation, and student well-being.

# 2. Preventive Maintenance Schedule for Electrical Apparatus

## 1. Battery Systems

- Check Voltage → Monthly

> Ensures batteries deliver the required output consistently.

- Check Voltage Alarm → Monthly

> Verifies that alarms for over/under-voltage conditions are functional.

- Specific Gravity and Plates → Annually

> Maintains chemical balance and structural integrity of lead-acid or similar battery types.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Prevents power failure during outages and ensures backup systems remain reliable.

## 2. Emergency Transfer Schemes

- Standby Generators – Run Up → Weekly

> Regular testing keeps generators in ready condition.

- Complete Transfer Test → Annually

> Simulates full system transfer to backup power, ensuring seamless emergency readiness.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Avoids system downtime in critical moments and validates generator reliability.

## 3. Main Circuit Breakers

- Operational Check → Monthly

> Confirms breakers function correctly and can interrupt circuits when required.

- Oil Inspection → Annually & Every 3 Years

> Checks insulation oil for contamination or degradation, preventing overheating and faults.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Prevents insulation breakdown, electrical fires, and catastrophic system failure.

## 4. Main Substation Transformers

- Temperature & Load Check → Monthly

> Verifies that transformers are not overloaded and operating within safe temperature limits.

- Oil Inspection → Annually & Every 3 Years

> Oil analysis detects contamination, ensuring insulation and cooling effectiveness.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Improves transformer lifespan, reduces overload risk, and avoids breakdowns.

## 5. Motors

- Bearings → Annually

> Inspected for wear to prevent failures.

- Greasing → Annually (or as required)

> Lubrication minimizes friction and overheating.

- Ventilation → Annually

> Ensures cooling systems are free from blockages.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Keeps motors energy efficient, prevents unexpected downtime, and extends service life.

## 6. Protection Equipment

- Cleaned and Checked for Calibration → Annually

> Maintains proper functioning of protective relays and devices.

- Kilowatt-hour Meters → Every 3 Years

> Calibrated to ensure accurate measurement of energy consumption.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Protects against electrical faults, ensures fair billing, and maintains compliance.

## 7. Substation – High Voltage

- Incoming Lines – Dirty Atmosphere → Annually

> Dust and pollution increase insulation breakdown risk, requiring frequent checks.

- Incoming Lines – Clean Areas → Every 2 Years

> Cleaner environments reduce the frequency of checks needed.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Ensures safety and uninterrupted power supply by preventing line faults.

## 8. Unit Substations

- Ground Indicators → Monthly

> Monitors grounding systems to avoid electrical shocks.

- Sump Pump → Monthly

> Prevents water accumulation and flooding in substations.

- Housekeeping → Monthly

> Ensures safe, clutter-free working environment.

- Temperature & Load Check → Annually

> Avoids overheating and overloading of local transformers.

- Oil Inspection (Transformers) → Annually & Every 3 Years

> Similar to main substation transformers, ensures insulation and cooling.
>
> **◆ Benefit:** Prevents accidents, maintains substation reliability, and ensures smooth operation.

## 9. Frequency Codes (PM Frequency)

| **Frequency**           | **Activities**                                                                     |
|-------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Weekly                  | Generator tests, sump pump checks.                                                 |
| Monthly                 | Voltage checks, breaker operational checks, transformer load checks, housekeeping. |
| Annually                | Oil inspections, motor maintenance, protective equipment calibration.              |
| Every 2 Years           | High-voltage incoming lines (clean areas).                                         |
| Every 3 Years           | Circuit breaker and transformer oil checks, meter calibration.                     |
| Change Only If Required | Greasing motors and other conditional replacements.                                |

## 10. Importance of Preventive Maintenance

- Reliability → Reduces the risk of sudden equipment failure.

- Safety → Protects workers, students, and infrastructure from electrical hazards.

- Longevity → Extends the service life of expensive electrical assets.

- Efficiency → Keeps energy distribution smooth and optimized.

- Compliance → Ensures adherence to industrial safety regulations and audit standards.

# 3. Preventive Maintenance Schedule for Electrical & ICT Apparatus in HEIs

## 1. UPS & Battery Backup Systems

- Check Voltage → Monthly

- Check Voltage Alarm → Monthly

- Specific Gravity / Battery Health Test → Annually

> ◆ Ensures uninterrupted power for servers, labs, and examination systems. Prevents power loss during outages.

## 2. Generators & Emergency Power

- Run Generator (No-load test) → Weekly

- Complete Power Transfer Simulation → Annually

> ◆ Keeps standby power supply reliable for classrooms, labs, and hostels during outages.

## 3. Classroom Projectors & Smart Boards

- Operational Check → Monthly

- Lamp/Filter Cleaning → Quarterly

- Deep Service / Calibration → Annually

> ◆ Ensures smart classrooms remain functional without interruptions.

## 4. Campus Transformers & Substations

- Temperature & Load Check → Monthly

- Oil Inspection & Analysis → Annually & Every 3 Years

> ◆ Protects against overload, overheating, and energy supply failures for hostels, labs, and admin offices.

## 5. Laboratory Equipment (Computers, Instruments, Servers)

- Hardware Check (RAM, Storage, Boot) → Monthly

- Dust Cleaning / Ventilation Check → Quarterly

- Calibration of Instruments → Annually

> ◆ Keeps labs (Physics, Chemistry, Computer Science, Engineering) safe and ready for academic/research work.

## 6. Solar Panels & Renewable Energy Units

- Cleaning of Panels → Monthly

- Inverter/Controller Check → Quarterly

- Performance & Energy Yield Audit → Annually

> ◆ Maintains energy efficiency, supports green campus initiatives, and ensures compliance with SDG goals.

## 7. Safety & Protection Equipment

- Fire Extinguishers (Check pressure & expiry) → Quarterly

- Electrical Protection Devices (ELCBs, MCBs) → Annually

- Energy Meters – Calibration → Every 3 Years

> ◆ Ensures safety and accurate energy monitoring across hostels, labs, and classrooms.

## 8. Networking & ICT Infrastructure

- Wi-Fi Access Points & Switches – Reboot/Test → Monthly

- Cabling & Rack Inspection → Quarterly

- Firewall/Server Backup Testing → Annually

> ◆ Keeps campus connectivity smooth, reliable, and cybersecure.

## 9. Hostel & Utility Equipment

- Water Pumps (Motor Check, Lubrication) → Monthly

- Sump Pump & Drainage → Monthly

- Electrical Wiring Inspection → Annually

> ◆ Ensures student services run without breakdowns.

### PM Frequency Codes for HEIs

| **Frequency** | **Activities**                                                                                                              |
|---------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Weekly        | Generator test runs, sump pump operation.                                                                                   |
| Monthly       | UPS checks, projector operation, Wi-Fi access point tests, solar panel cleaning.                                            |
| Quarterly     | Filter cleaning (projectors/AC), lab dust removal, fire extinguisher inspection.                                            |
| Annually      | Calibration of lab instruments, deep cleaning/service of projectors, oil inspection for transformers, firewall/server test. |
| Every 3 Years | Transformer oil deep check, energy meter calibration, replacement of end-of-life UPS batteries.                             |
| As Required   | Lamp/toner replacement, grease for motors, IT equipment upgrades.                                                           |

### Benefits for HEIs

- Reliability → Smooth academic operations (smart classrooms, labs, hostels).

- Safety → Fire, electrical, and cyber risks reduced.

- Longevity → Equipment lifespan extended, saving costs.

- Sustainability → Supports NAAC/NBA audits, SDGs (clean energy, responsible consumption).

- Compliance → Provides verifiable documentation for IQAC, Green Audits, and accreditation bodies.

# 4. Electrical Maintenance and Operation Process Checklist

What this checklist is

- A structured review of everything needed to manage electrical assets effectively.

- Divided into process areas (numbered 1–11), each covering a major domain (documentation, training, maintenance, safety, etc.).

- Each lettered item (A, B, C, etc.) is a control point to verify with evidence (manuals, reports, calibration certificates, etc.).

- Helps institutions avoid accidents, outages, regulatory penalties, and inefficiency.

## 1. Documentation & Drawings

- **As-built drawings:** Must reflect the current state of the system, easily accessible.

- **Instruction manuals:** Available in labs, substations, and control rooms.

- **Revision control:** After every modification, manuals/drawings are updated with approval stamps, revision logs, and version numbers.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Prevents errors during maintenance and ensures new staff can operate equipment safely.

## 2. Maintenance Program Design & Criticality

- **Maintenance plan:** Defines scope, frequency, methods, responsibilities.

- **Evidence of execution:** Completed preventive maintenance (PM) checklists, sign-offs, backlog reports.

- **Updates after change:** Linked to *Management of Change (MOC)* system.

- **Critical equipment:** Identified and prioritized (e.g., UPS for data center, substation transformers).

- **Work controls:** Lockout/Tagout, permits, supervision.

- **Redundancy:** Configurations like N+1 ensure continuous service.

- **Condition-based adjustment:** Test results like insulation resistance, DGA (oil analysis), breaker contact resistance trend over time.

- **Repair vs. replace decisions:** Based on life-cycle cost, downtime risks, and spares availability.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Keeps essential teaching, research, and campus operations uninterrupted.

## 3. Technical Training & Competence

- **Formal training program:** Curriculum, refresher courses, periodic testing.

- **Training records:** Up-to-date logs of who has been trained and on what.

- **Needs analysis:** Identifies training gaps.

- **Program review:** Updates content for new hazards or technologies.

- **Test instruments:** Available, fit-for-purpose, and within calibration.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Competent staff reduce risks of accidents and costly mistakes.

## 4. System Studies & Protective Devices

- **Short-circuit & coordination studies:** Must reflect current one-line diagram.

- **Protective devices:** Tested periodically (relays, breakers, fuses).

- **Revalidation:** After any major system change or utility supply variation.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Ensures devices trip correctly to prevent large-scale blackouts or equipment damage.

## 5. Arc-Flash & PPE Management

- **Arc-flash studies:** Performed and labels posted on equipment.

- **PPE categories:** Clearly marked (face shield, gloves, arc suits).

- **Updates:** Required when system capacity or fault levels change.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Protects technicians from life-threatening electrical incidents.

## 6. Load & Power-Quality Monitoring

- **Load monitoring:** Logs of demand, feeder loads, transformer performance.

- **Power quality:** Monitors harmonics, voltage sags/swells.

- **Corrective actions:** Derating, balancing, filters as needed.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Reduces outages, protects sensitive lab equipment, ensures energy efficiency.

## 7. Unplanned-Outage Risk & Investigation

- **Cost awareness:** Staff trained on consequences (safety, academic loss, fines).

- **Other liabilities:** Environmental violations, missed research/project deadlines.

- **Root Cause Analysis (RCA):** Formal investigations after outages.

- **Feedback loop:** Results used to improve design, maintenance, and training.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Prevents repeat failures and ensures accountability.

## 8. Environmental Compliance

- **Inclusion in environmental manual:** Especially for oil-filled transformers, batteries.

- **Worker training:** Spill response, waste handling.

- **Audits:** Physical verification of containment, labeling, disposal manifests.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Protects environment and ensures regulatory compliance.

## 9. Hazardous-Area Identification

- **Drawings:** Hazardous zones marked (labs, chemical storage).

- **Signage:** Physical warnings in place.

- **Updates:** After modifications to layouts or installations.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Prevents ignition of flammable areas, aligns with safety codes.

## 10. Electrical Safety Manual & Work Practices

- **Work procedures:** LOTO, energized work permits, absence-of-voltage testing.

- **Manual updates:** Reflect code changes and lessons learned.

- **Corrective actions:** Incidents documented and closed.

- **Training & assessments:** To ensure workers follow procedures.

- **Audits:** Supervisors verify compliance in field conditions.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Enforces a culture of safety and accountability.

## 11. Emergency Repair & Spares Readiness

- **Emergency repair plan:** Lists critical assets and action steps.

- **Contact lists:** Contractors, suppliers, utilities.

- **Spares program:** Controlled inventory, shelf-life checks.

- **Updates:** After equipment additions/removals.

<!-- -->

- *Why*: Enables fast recovery and minimizes downtime.

### Evidence Required

- Controlled drawings, PM records, calibration certificates, training logs, arc-flash labels, PQ data, RCA reports, environmental permits, safety audits, spare inventory, emergency call trees.

### Typical Gaps

- Outdated one-lines, expired PPE labels, missing calibration, uncontrolled spares, training lapses, studies not updated post-upgrade, undocumented settings changes.

### How to Adapt for HEIs

- **Scope:** Campus substations, UPS systems in data centers, smart classroom panels, lab DBs, hostel power systems.

- **Audit tie-in:** Link to ICT/Facilities Monthly Reports.

- **Accreditation evidence:** Use as NAAC/NBA/NIRF/ISO documentation.

- **Registers:** Connect corrective actions to Preventive Maintenance Register & Monthly Physical Facilities Maintenance Report.

# 5. Building Maintenance

## 1. Introduction

- Building maintenance refers to the systematic care, repair, and upkeep of institutional infrastructure to ensure safety, efficiency, and usability.

- In HEIs, it covers classrooms, laboratories, hostels, libraries, auditoriums, administrative offices, ICT facilities, and utility systems.

- It is both preventive (routine inspections to avoid failures) and corrective (repairs after damage or breakdown).

## 2. Objectives of Building Maintenance

- Safety → Ensuring structural, electrical, and fire safety for students, faculty, and staff.

- Functionality → Maintaining classrooms, labs, ICT infrastructure, and utilities in working condition.

- Longevity → Extending the life of assets by timely care.

- Compliance → Meeting regulatory standards, building codes, and accreditation requirements (NAAC, NBA, ISO, SDGs).

- Sustainability → Reducing resource wastage (energy, water) and promoting green practices.

## 3. Types of Building Maintenance

### A. Preventive Maintenance

- Scheduled checks (monthly, quarterly, annually).

- **Examples:** roof inspections, pest control, HVAC servicing, electrical safety audits.

### B. Corrective Maintenance

- Repairs carried out after failure or damage.

- **Examples:** fixing a leaking pipe, replacing broken glass, repairing faulty wiring.

### C. Predictive Maintenance

- Using sensors and monitoring tools to predict failures before they occur.

- **Examples:** IoT-based HVAC monitoring, transformer oil analysis.

## 4. Key Areas of Building Maintenance

### A. Structural Maintenance

- Exterior Walls → Ensure cleanliness, no cracks, pest-free.

- Roof → Check leaks, vents, flashings, and access safety.

- Doors & Windows → Condition, locks, weather-stripping, hinges.

### B. Electrical Systems

- Panels & Receptacles → Covers secure, no exposed wiring.

- UPS/Generators → Regular load tests.

- Smart Classrooms → Projector and ICT equipment maintenance.

- Campus Lighting → Energy-efficient fixtures, emergency lighting checks.

### C. Plumbing & Water Systems

- Water Faucets & Fixtures → Leak checks, winterization where needed.

- Drainage → Free from blockages.

- Sanitation → Proper functioning of toilets and washrooms.

### D. Furniture & Fixtures

- Seats/Tables → Ensure no sharp edges, stable and safe.

- Lab Benches → Resistant to chemicals, wear, and tear.

### E. Safety & Compliance

- Fire Safety → Fire extinguishers charged, alarms tested, evacuation routes marked.

- Hazardous Materials Handling → For labs, proper storage and disposal.

- Emergency Plans → Updated repair contacts, spares inventory, call lists.

## 5. Building Maintenance Process

**1. Inspection & Audit**

- Monthly/quarterly checklists for classrooms, labs, and utilities.

- Specialized audits (electrical, fire safety, environmental).

**2. Reporting**

- Issues logged in a Maintenance Register (manual or digital).

- Classified by urgency (High/Medium/Low).

**3. Action & Repair**

- Task assigned to responsible department (engineering, IT cell, housekeeping).

- Repairs documented with date and cost.

**4. Verification & Closure**

- Issue re-inspected and closed with a signature by verifying authority.

**5. Review & Planning**

- Annual report created for IQAC/Facilities Committee.

- Used for budget planning and accreditation evidence.

## 6. Accreditation & Compliance Linkages

- NAAC Criterion 4 → Infrastructure and Learning Resources.

- NBA Criterion 7 → Facilities and Technical Support.

- NIRF TLR → Teaching, Learning, and Resources (availability of functional facilities).

- QS I·GAUGE Facilities & Governance → Emphasis on physical infrastructure and sustainability.

- SDGs → Especially Goal 9 (Infrastructure), Goal 11 (Sustainable Communities), and Goal 12 (Responsible Consumption).

## 7. Benefits of Proper Building Maintenance

- Ensures safe, functional learning spaces for students and staff.

- Minimizes costly emergency repairs through preventive care.

- Enhances institutional reputation and student satisfaction.

- Supports energy efficiency and sustainability commitments.

- Provides documented evidence for quality audits and accreditations.

## 8. Tools & Records

- Building Maintenance Checklist (monthly inspection sheet like the one you uploaded).

- Maintenance Compliance Register (records issues, actions, closure dates).

- Monthly/Annual Audit Reports (for management and IQAC).

- Digital Facility Management Systems (FMS) → For large campuses.

## Building Maintenance Checklist

### 1. Header Section

- Year & Location → Identifies which building/site is being monitored and for which calendar year.

- Ensures traceability when records are stored for annual audits, NAAC/NBA facility reports, or ISO compliance.

### 2. Layout

- **Columns:** Months (January – December) → Each month has a column where inspections are recorded.

- **Rows:** Building Components (Exterior walls, Doors, Windows, Roof, etc.) with specific checkpoints listed under each.

- **Entries are typically marked as:**

<!-- -->

- [x] (Good condition/Compliant)

- ✗ (Issue detected)

- N/A (Not applicable for that facility)

### 3. Categories of Inspection

A. Exterior Walls

- Clean → Free of dirt, moss, mold, or graffiti.

- Flashings/Secure → Properly sealed to avoid water seepage.

- Good Condition → No cracks, bulges, or structural damage.

- No Pest/Wasp/etc. → Check for nests, infestations.

- Other → Any unusual findings (e.g., water stains, loose plaster).

◆ *Importance*: Maintains structural integrity, prevents pest hazards, and ensures a safe external appearance.

B. Doors

- Good Condition → No warping, cracks, or rot.

- Secure Hardware/Handles → Functioning properly without looseness.

- Locks Working → Tested for security (especially in labs, classrooms, storage).

- Glass Good Condition → For doors with glass panels, no cracks.

- Other → E.g., automatic closers, accessibility features.

◆ *Importance*: Ensures safety, accessibility, and security of building spaces.

C. Windows

- Good Condition → Frames intact, no broken glass.

- Latches in Place → Locking mechanisms work.

- Weather-stripping Good → Prevents drafts and water leakage.

- Hinges Secure → No rusting or loose fittings.

◆ *Importance*: Energy efficiency, ventilation, and safety compliance.

D. Roof

- Good Condition → No sagging, cracked tiles, or structural weakness.

- Flashings Secure → Prevents water leakage at joints/edges.

- Vents Clear → No obstructions in air vents.

- Access Clear → Safe pathway for maintenance staff.

- No Evident Leaks → Ceiling inspections to detect water marks.

- Other → E.g., bird-proofing, antenna fixtures.

◆ *Importance*: Prevents water damage, structural deterioration, and indoor hazards.

E. Electric Panels/Receptacles

- Covers in Place → No open wiring, panels securely closed.

- Nothing Left Plugged In → Prevents overheating/fire risk.

◆ *Importance*: Electrical safety and compliance with IT/electrical audit standards.

F. Water Faucets

- Not Leaking → Reduces water wastage and slip hazards.

- Winterized → Pipes insulated to prevent freezing (for cold regions).

◆ *Importance*: Utility efficiency, sustainability (SDG-6 compliance).

G. Seats/Tables

- Good Condition → Classroom and outdoor furniture checked for stability, no sharp edges, no damage.

◆ *Importance*: Comfort, safety, and usability for students and staff.

### 4. How it Works in Practice

- Each month, the maintenance officer/IT cell/facility manager inspects the listed items.

- Records are entered in the corresponding month’s cell (✓/✗/remarks).

- Any issues found are reported in a Maintenance Action Register (with corrective action details, responsible team, and closure date).

- **At year-end, this sheet provides evidence of continuous monitoring for:**

<!-- -->

- **NAAC (Criterion 4:** Infrastructure & Learning Resources)

- NBA (Facilities & Technical Support)

- NIRF (Teaching-Learning Resources)

- ISO/QS I·GAUGE Facility Audits

### 5. Benefits

- Structured Monitoring → Ensures no aspect of building upkeep is missed.

- Accountability → Departments know when and where issues were reported.

- Preventive Maintenance → Small issues (like leaks) fixed before becoming major repairs.

- Audit-Ready Evidence → Serves as proof of systematic building care.

# 6. Policies Related to Physical Facilities, Maintenance & Construction

## 1. Physical Facilities Policies

- Infrastructure Development Policy – outlines planning, allocation, and upgrading of academic, research, and student facilities.

- Space Allocation Policy – ensures equitable distribution of classrooms, labs, offices, hostels, and common areas.

- Green Campus Policy – promotes eco-friendly practices (energy efficiency, water conservation, e-waste, solar power).

- ICT Facility Policy – ensures provision and upkeep of digital infrastructure (Wi-Fi, smart classrooms, LMS, ERP).

- Accessibility Policy – provides inclusive design for differently-abled persons (ramps, lifts, signage).

## 2. Maintenance Policies

- Preventive Maintenance Policy – defines periodic inspections for buildings, electrical, water, and ICT facilities.

- Corrective & Emergency Maintenance Policy – covers immediate response to breakdowns or hazards.

- Annual Maintenance Contract (AMC) Policy – ensures third-party servicing of ICT equipment, HVAC, elevators, lab equipment.

- Building Safety & Security Policy – ensures fire safety, electrical safety, structural safety, and compliance with codes.

- Energy & Utilities Management Policy – monitoring of electricity, water, HVAC, generators, and renewable sources.

- Waste Management & Recycling Policy – includes solid waste, biomedical waste (labs/clinics), and e-waste disposal.

## 3. Construction Policies

- Master Plan & Campus Development Policy – long-term roadmap for expansion, zoning, and sustainability.

- Building Codes & Standards Compliance Policy – ensures alignment with NBC (National Building Code), local municipal rules, and fire safety norms.

- Construction Procurement & Tendering Policy – transparent guidelines for vendor selection and project execution.

- Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) Policy – mandates environmental clearance for large-scale construction.

- Sustainable Construction Policy – emphasizes green materials, renewable energy integration, and smart building technologies.

- Capital Expenditure & Budget Allocation Policy – defines financial approval process for new buildings and renovations.

## 4. Safety & Emergency Policies

- Disaster Management & Emergency Response Policy – preparedness for fire, earthquake, flood, or electrical failures.

- Occupational Health & Safety (OHS) Policy – safety of maintenance workers, contractors, and staff during facility operations.

- Hazardous Materials Handling Policy – especially for laboratories (chemicals, gases, radioactive materials).

## 5. Compliance & Audit Policies

- Facility Audit & Inspection Policy – regular audits of classrooms, labs, hostels, utilities, and ICT facilities.

- Accreditation Compliance Policy – ensures facilities meet NAAC/NBA/NIRF/QS I·GAUGE benchmarks.

- Documentation & Record-Keeping Policy – registers for building maintenance, ICT facilities, obsolete equipment, safety checks, etc.

## 6. Governance & Accountability Policies

- IT & Facility Management Cell Formation Policy – defines roles/responsibilities of facility management committees.

- Delegation of Financial Powers for Maintenance Policy – assigns authority to approve repairs/construction.

- Vendor & Contractor Accountability Policy – ensures adherence to timelines, quality standards, and safety rules.

# 7. Master Plan & Campus Development Policy

## 1. Introduction

- A Master Plan is a long-term roadmap for guiding the physical growth and infrastructure development of a university or HEI.

- It integrates academic priorities, student growth, sustainability, inclusivity, safety, and technology adoption into campus expansion.

- This policy ensures that the campus evolves in line with the institution’s vision & mission, accreditation standards, and sustainable development principles.

## 2. Objectives

- To provide a structured framework for campus growth and infrastructure modernization.

- To align space, utilities, and infrastructure with projected student and faculty strength.

- To adopt sustainable practices in construction, energy, water, and waste management.

- To ensure universal accessibility and inclusivity for all stakeholders.

- To create a smart campus integrating ICT, IoT, and digital technologies.

- To provide evidence-based documentation for accreditation and quality audits.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- Academic facilities (classrooms, laboratories, libraries, research centers).

- Student amenities (hostels, canteens, health centers, sports complexes).

- Administrative facilities (departments, offices, exam cells).

- Digital infrastructure (data centers, smart classrooms, LMS/ERP).

- Utilities & services (water, power, sanitation, transport, landscaping).

- Green & sustainable infrastructure (solar, rainwater harvesting, waste management).

## 4. Guiding Principles

- Alignment with Institutional Vision – Infrastructure must directly support teaching, research, and extension activities.

- Sustainability – Adoption of green buildings, renewable energy, zero-waste management, and biodiversity protection.

- Inclusivity & Accessibility – Universal design standards for PwDs, gender-sensitive facilities, and safe spaces.

- Phased Development – Expansion based on student intake, new programs, and research growth.

- Integrated Infrastructure – Multipurpose facilities (auditoriums, ICT labs) for efficient use of resources.

- Resilience & Safety – Compliance with fire safety, disaster management, seismic codes, and cybersecurity norms.

## 5. Governance & Responsibility

- **Campus Development Committee (CDC):**

<!-- -->

- **Chair:** Dean (Planning & Development).

- **Members:** Registrar, Heads of Departments, Finance Officer, Facilities Manager, IQAC representative, Student Council nominee.

<!-- -->

- **Responsibilities:**

<!-- -->

- Prepare and update the Master Plan (10–20 year horizon).

- Prioritize projects annually based on academic and institutional needs.

- Monitor implementation and ensure compliance with regulations.

- Report progress to Governing Body / Board of Management.

## 6. Implementation Framework

**1. Needs Assessment – Collect input from departments, students, and stakeholders.**

**2. Master Plan Preparation – Define zoning (academic, residential, recreational, green).**

**3. Phasing & Budgeting – Short-term (1–3 years), medium-term (3–7 years), long-term (7–20 years).**

**4. Execution – Construction through transparent tendering and vendor selection.**

**5. Monitoring – Quarterly progress reviews by CDC and IQAC.**

**6. Documentation – Maintain registers, project files, architectural drawings, and approvals.**

## 7. Accreditation & Compliance Linkages

- NAAC Criterion 4 – Physical facilities & infrastructure adequacy.

- NBA Criterion 7 – Technical support & adequacy of infrastructure for OBE.

- NIRF TLR – Teaching-learning resources linked to infrastructure quality.

- QS I·GAUGE – Campus development, governance, and sustainability benchmarks.

- SDGs – SDG 9 (Infrastructure), SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities & Communities), SDG 13 (Climate Action).

## 8. Review & Upgradation

- The Master Plan will be reviewed every 5 years, or earlier if major expansions are needed.

- Updates will incorporate new academic programs, student growth, technology upgrades, and sustainability trends.

- Purpose of Allocation → Intended usage (lab, classroom, faculty office, seminar hall).

- Allocation Type → Whether it is a *New allocation* or *Re-allocation* (repurposing).

- Date of Allocation → Date space was approved/assigned.

- Responsible Officer / Dept. → Facilities Manager, Registrar, HOD, Estate Officer.

- Remarks → Notes on modifications, pending approvals, or special usage conditions.

### Facilities Provided to Functionaries – Register

| **Serial Number** | **Facility Number** | **Facility Description** | **Functionaries Allocated (HOD / Dean / Faculty / Non-Teaching / Administrative Staff / Others)** | **Location (Building / Block / Office / Department)** | **Date of Allocation** | **Responsible Officer / Dept.** | **Verified By (Registrar / IQAC / Estate Office)** | **Remarks** |

Column Explanation

- Serial Number → Running record of entries.

- Facility Number → Unique ID for each facility (e.g., *FAC/2025/01*).

- Facility Description → Type of facility provided (e.g., *Office cabin with furniture, Laptop, Internet access*).

- Functionaries Allocated → Who is assigned (HOD, Dean, Faculty, Administrative Officer, Staff).

- Location → Specific building/department where facility is located.

- Date of Allocation → Date when facility was allocated.

- Responsible Officer / Dept. → Estate Office, IT Cell, Facilities Manager.

- Verified By → Registrar, IQAC, or Governing Body.

- Remarks → Notes on condition, upgrades, or reallocation.

| **Serial Number** | **Facility Number** | **Facility Description** | **Department Allocated** | **Date Allocated** | **% Utilisation (Avg. per Month)** | **Department Reallocated To (if applicable)** | **Date of Reallocation** | **Responsible Officer / Dept.** | **Verified By (Registrar / IQAC / Estate Office)** | **Remarks** |

Column Explanation

- Serial Number → Running record.

- Facility Number → Unique code for each facility (e.g., *FAC/2025/01*).

- Facility Description → Type of facility (lab, classroom, seminar hall, IT lab, office room, equipment).

- Department Allocated → Which department initially received the facility.

- Date Allocated → When facility was assigned.

- % Utilisation → Average usage (based on schedules, ERP logs, lab booking systems, or audits).

- Department Reallocated To → If shifted, new department name.

- Date of Reallocation → When facility was re-assigned.

- Responsible Officer / Dept. → Facilities Manager, Registrar, HoD, Estate Officer.

- Verified By → Registrar, IQAC, Governing Body.

- Remarks → Notes on under-utilization, repurposing, or AMC status.

### Infrastructural Facilities & Gap Analysis Register – As on \[DD/MM/YYYY\]

| **Serial Number** | **Infrastructure Number** | **Type of Infrastructure (Classroom / Lab / Library / Hostel / Sports / Office / Other)** | **Plinth Area (Sq. ft)** | **Carpet Area (Sq. ft)** | **Seating Capacity** | **Gadget Serial** | **Type of Gadget (PC / Projector / Lab Equipment / AC / Other)** | **Number of Gadgets** | **Area Required as per Norms (Sq. ft)** | **GAP in Area (Available – Required)** | **Compliance Status (Yes / No / Partial)** | **Responsible Dept. / Officer** | **Remarks** |
|-------------------|---------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------|
|                   |                           |                                                                                           |                          |                          |                      |                   |                                                                  |                       |                                         |                                        |                                            |                                 |             |

Column Explanation

- Infrastructure Number → Unique code for each facility (e.g., *INF/2025/01*).

- Type of Infrastructure → Facility type (classroom, lab, library, hostel, office, etc.).

- Plinth Area → Built-up area.

- Carpet Area → Usable space.

- Seating Capacity → Number of students/staff accommodated.

- Gadget Serial & Type → Equipment allocated (computers, projectors, ACs, lab instruments).

- Number of Gadgets → Count of devices available.

- Area Required as per Norms → UGC/AICTE/NAAC/NBA minimum requirement.

- GAP in Area → Shortage/Excess = *Available – Required*.

- Compliance Status → Yes (Meets norms), No (Gap exists), Partial (Partially meets norms).

- Responsible Dept. / Officer → Facilities Manager, HOD, Estate Officer.

- Remarks → Notes on deficiencies, plans for expansion, or reallocation.

# 8. Campus Facilities & Infrastructure in HEIs

## 1. Academic Facilities

- Classrooms

<!-- -->

- Equipped with ergonomic seating, projectors, whiteboards, and smart boards.

- Designed for blended learning with ICT-enabled setups (Wi-Fi, streaming).

- Adequate ventilation, lighting, and acoustics for effective learning.

- **Accessibility features:** ramps, wide corridors, seating for PwDs.

<!-- -->

- Laboratories

<!-- -->

- Specialized labs for engineering, sciences, medical, and vocational studies.

- Equipped with modern instruments, simulation software, IoT-enabled devices.

- Safety provisions (fume hoods, fire extinguishers, eyewash stations).

- Lab manuals, SOPs, and periodic maintenance schedules.

<!-- -->

- Libraries

<!-- -->

- **Physical:** Well-ventilated, digital catalogue, reading rooms.

- **Digital:** E-journals, databases (IEEE, Springer, Scopus), e-books.

- Facilities for differently-abled learners (screen readers, braille).

- Integrated with Institutional Repository (IR) for theses and publications.

<!-- -->

- Research Centers

<!-- -->

- High-end research labs (AI/ML labs, biotech centers, innovation hubs).

- Access to High-Performance Computing (HPC) and GPU clusters.

- Collaboration spaces for interdisciplinary research.

- Support for patents, incubations, and funded projects.

## 2. Student Amenities

- Hostels

<!-- -->

- Safe, hygienic accommodation with Wi-Fi and study spaces.

- **Facilities:** dining halls, common rooms, laundry, solar heating, water purifiers.

- Gender-sensitive infrastructure and wardens.

<!-- -->

- Canteens & Food Courts

<!-- -->

- Affordable, hygienic meals.

- Options for vegetarian/vegan, diet-conscious, and cultural diversity.

- Waste segregation and composting for sustainability.

<!-- -->

- Health Centers

<!-- -->

- Basic healthcare with doctors/nurses on campus.

- Tie-ups with local hospitals for emergencies.

- Facilities for first-aid, vaccination, and mental health counseling.

<!-- -->

- Sports Complexes

<!-- -->

- **Indoor & outdoor facilities:** gym, yoga halls, courts, fields, swimming pools.

- Support for national/international student competitions.

- Provision of adaptive sports for differently-abled students.

## 3. Administrative Facilities

- Departments & Offices

<!-- -->

- Dedicated spaces for each academic department.

- Offices for faculty, administrative staff, and departmental libraries.

- Equipped with ICT tools (ERP terminals, printers, LAN).

<!-- -->

- Examination Cell

<!-- -->

- Secure digital storage of question papers and exam records.

- Facilities for digital evaluation, online exams, plagiarism checks.

- Biometric or smart ID-based student verification systems.

<!-- -->

- Registrar & Governance Offices

<!-- -->

- Transparent records management with ERP integration.

- Student service centers (admissions, fee payment, document verification).

## 4. Digital Infrastructure

- Data Centers

<!-- -->

- Institutional servers for ERP, LMS, research storage, email systems.

- Equipped with backup, firewall, IDS/IPS, and cloud integration.

- Disaster recovery and redundancy mechanisms.

<!-- -->

- Smart Classrooms

<!-- -->

- ICT-enabled with interactive boards, cameras for hybrid teaching.

- Lecture capture and streaming systems integrated with LMS.

<!-- -->

- Learning Management Systems (LMS)

<!-- -->

- Platforms like Moodle, Blackboard, SWAYAM, or custom ERP.

- **Features:** course content, quizzes, assignments, analytics, student feedback.

<!-- -->

- Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP)

<!-- -->

- **Modules:** admissions, attendance, exams, HR, finance, hostels, transport.

- Integration with Academic Bank of Credits (ABC) for NEP-2020 compliance.

## 5. Utilities & Services

- Water Supply & Sanitation

<!-- -->

- Continuous clean water supply with purification and storage.

- Water-saving fixtures and dual-flush systems.

- Sanitation facilities as per Swachh Bharat Mission norms.

<!-- -->

- Power Supply

<!-- -->

- Redundant power with campus-wide UPS and generator backup.

- Energy-efficient lighting (LED, motion sensors).

<!-- -->

- Transport

<!-- -->

- Buses, vans, and e-shuttles for students and staff.

- Accessible transport for PwDs.

- EV charging stations for sustainable mobility.

<!-- -->

- Landscaping & Campus Environment

<!-- -->

- Green belts, herbal gardens, biodiversity parks.

- Spaces for student interaction, cultural events, and recreation.

## 6. Green & Sustainable Infrastructure

- Renewable Energy (Solar/Wind/Biomass)

<!-- -->

- Rooftop solar panels for classrooms, hostels, labs.

- Solar water heaters in hostels.

<!-- -->

- Rainwater Harvesting

<!-- -->

- Recharge pits and underground tanks.

- Integrated with campus water supply for reuse.

<!-- -->

- Waste Management

<!-- -->

- Solid waste segregation (biodegradable, recyclable, e-waste, biomedical).

- Composting units for food waste.

- Certified e-waste disposal partners.

<!-- -->

- Green Buildings

<!-- -->

- Energy-efficient design following Green Building Code.

- Use of natural lighting, ventilation, and eco-friendly materials.

- Smart IoT-based energy monitoring.

### Campus Facilities Compliance Register

| **S. No.** | **Facility Category (Academic / Student / Admin / Digital / Utilities / Sustainability)** | **Department / Location** | **Facility / Component** | **Compliance Standard / Benchmark** | **Compliance Status (Yes / No / Partial)** | **Issues Identified** | **Action Taken / Proposed** | **Responsible Team / Committee** | **Verified By (IQAC / CDC / Facility Committee)** | **Remarks** |
|------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------|
|            |                                                                                           |                           |                          |                                     |                                            |                       |                             |                                  |                                                   |             |

Column Explanation

- **Facility Category → One of six:** Academic, Student, Admin, Digital, Utilities, Sustainability.

- Department / Location → E.g., Physics Lab, Commerce Block, Hostel A, Library, Auditorium.

- **Facility / Component → Specific facility:** Smart Classroom, Library Database, Hostel Room, Data Center, Solar Plant, Transport Service.

- **Compliance Standard / Benchmark → Reference:** NAAC, NBA, UGC, AICTE norms, Green Building Code, WCAG, RPwD Act, Fire Safety Codes.

- **Compliance Status → Drop-down:** Yes / No / Partial.

- Issues Identified → Gaps found during audits (e.g., projector not working, water leak, ERP license expired).

- Action Taken / Proposed → Repairs, upgrades, AMC renewal, vendor engagement.

- Responsible Team / Committee → Engineering Dept., IT Cell, Hostel Admin, Library, Green Campus Committee, Exam Cell.

- Verified By → IQAC, Campus Development Committee (CDC), External Auditors.

- **Remarks → Notes:** pending budget, work in progress, critical priority, etc.

### Canteen Maintenance Register

| **Serial Number** | **MS Number (Maintenance Schedule Ref.)** | **Type of Maintenance (Plumbing / Electrical / Kitchen Equipment / Hygiene / Renovation / AMC / Other)** | **Date of Maintenance Scheduled** | **Description of Maintenance** | **Estimated Cost of Maintenance (₹)** | **Date of Maintenance Undertaken** | **Responsible Person / Dept.** | **Verified By (Facilities / Hostel Warden / IQAC)** | **Remarks** |
|-------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------|---------------------------------------|------------------------------------|--------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|-------------|

Column Explanation

- Serial Number → Running record of maintenance works.

- MS Number → Unique reference number for each scheduled maintenance activity.

- Type of Maintenance → Category (plumbing, electrical, kitchen equipment, hygiene, pest control, furniture).

- Date of Maintenance Scheduled → Planned date of work.

- Description of Maintenance → Details (e.g., *deep cleaning of kitchen exhausts, repair of gas pipeline*).

- Estimated Cost of Maintenance (₹) → Cost projection.

- Date of Maintenance Undertaken → Actual date of execution.

- Responsible Person / Dept. → Canteen Manager, Facilities Dept., Hostel Warden.

- Verified By → IQAC, Registrar, or Food Safety Officer.

- Remarks → Notes on delays, vendor performance, AMC coverage.

# 9. Accessibility Policy

## 1. Introduction

- Accessibility is a fundamental principle of higher education, ensuring that all members of the university community — students, faculty, staff, and visitors, including persons with disabilities (PwDs) — have equal opportunity to access, participate, and benefit from academic and non-academic facilities.

- **This policy aligns with:**

<!-- -->

- Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016

- University Grants Commission (UGC) Guidelines

- National Education Policy (NEP) 2020

- Accreditation frameworks (NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE)

- UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 4, 10, 11)

## 2. Objectives

- To provide inclusive infrastructure that supports universal accessibility.

- To remove physical, digital, and academic barriers for differently-abled persons.

- To ensure compliance with national laws and accreditation standards.

- To sensitize the campus community about accessibility and inclusivity.

- To continuously improve facilities through feedback and audits.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- **Physical Infrastructure:** Classrooms, labs, libraries, hostels, offices, toilets, common spaces.

- **ICT & Digital Infrastructure:** Websites, LMS, ERP, e-library, mobile apps.

- **Student & Staff Services:** Admissions, examinations, transport, counseling, career services.

- **Community Engagement:** Events, seminars, workshops, outreach programs.

## 4. Key Provisions

### A. Physical Accessibility

- Ramps, elevators, tactile paths, wide corridors for wheelchair mobility.

- Accessible toilets in all major buildings.

- Signage in braille and large print.

- Reserved seating in classrooms, libraries, and auditoriums.

- Hostel facilities designed with accessibility features.

### B. ICT & Digital Accessibility

- University website compliant with WCAG 2.1 standards (screen-reader compatibility, captions, alt-text for images).

- Accessible e-learning platforms (LMS, MOOCs, ERP).

- Assistive technology support (screen readers, speech-to-text, magnification tools).

- Availability of digital course content in multiple formats (audio, e-text, large print, braille).

### C. Academic Accessibility

- Flexibility in assessment and examinations (scribe facility, extra time, alternative evaluation).

- Provision of accessible study materials (braille, e-books, recorded lectures).

- Inclusive teaching-learning practices (universal design for learning).

- Dedicated resource center for PwDs with assistive technologies.

### D. Transportation & Mobility

- Accessible campus transport (ramps, hydraulic lifts).

- Reserved parking spaces near academic and administrative blocks.

### E. Sensitization & Training

- Awareness workshops for faculty, staff, and students on inclusivity.

- Training for administrative staff in handling accessibility-related needs.

### F. Governance & Monitoring

- Accessibility Committee chaired by Dean (Student Welfare / Planning & Development).

- Inclusion of PwD representatives in decision-making.

- Annual accessibility audit of physical and digital facilities.

- Feedback mechanism for PwDs to report issues.

## 5. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- **NAAC Criterion 4 & 7:** Infrastructure inclusivity, student support, best practices.

- **NBA Criterion 7:** Adequacy of facilities, equity of access.

- **NIRF Outreach & Inclusivity (OI):** Accessibility for differently-abled students.

- **QS I·GAUGE Learner Diversity & Inclusivity:** Infrastructure and support for PwDs.

- **SDGs:**

<!-- -->

- **SDG 4:** Inclusive and equitable education.

- **SDG 10:** Reducing inequalities.

- **SDG 11:** Sustainable and inclusive communities.

## 6. Review of Policy

- The Accessibility Policy will be reviewed every 2 years.

- Updates will reflect changes in laws, accreditation frameworks, and stakeholder feedback.

# 10. ICT Facility Policy

## 1. Introduction

- ICT is the backbone of modern higher education and an essential enabler of teaching–learning, research, governance, and communication.

- This policy provides guidelines for the creation, management, security, and responsible use of ICT facilities at the institution.

- It ensures compliance with NAAC (Criterion 4 & 6), NBA (Facilities & Technical Support), NIRF (Teaching-Learning Resources), QS I·GAUGE (ICT Facilities) and is aligned with NEP-2020.

## 2. Objectives

- To provide reliable and secure ICT infrastructure for all academic and administrative stakeholders.

- To ensure equitable access to ICT resources for students, faculty, researchers, and staff.

- To promote digital transformation and e-governance in institutional operations.

- To support blended learning, MOOCs, and digital classrooms.

- To establish standards for security, maintenance, and usage of ICT resources.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- **ICT Infrastructure:** Servers, networks, Wi-Fi, firewalls, data centers, UPS.

- **End-User Devices:** Desktops, laptops, tablets, projectors, printers.

- **Software & Applications:** LMS (Moodle, Blackboard, SWAYAM), ERP systems, SIS, digital library access, licensed software.

- **Digital Learning Facilities:** Smart classrooms, hybrid learning setups, video conferencing systems.

- **Communication Systems:** University email, collaboration tools (MS Teams, Zoom, Google Meet).

- **Users:** Students, faculty, non-teaching staff, research scholars, and authorized visitors.

## 4. Governance & Responsibility

- **ICT Facility Committee / IT Cell:**

<!-- -->

- Responsible for ICT planning, procurement, deployment, and review.

- Chaired by CIO/Dean (IT/Planning & Development).

<!-- -->

- **System Administrators:** Maintain servers, networks, LMS, ERP, and provide user support.

- **Departments/Units:** Ensure proper use of ICT facilities in labs, classrooms, and offices.

- **IQAC:** Audit ICT services and maintain evidence for accreditation.

## 5. ICT Facility Standards

### A. Access & Usage

- All users must authenticate using institutional credentials (ERP/LMS/Email IDs).

- Access to ICT facilities will be role-based (student, faculty, admin, researcher).

- Users must comply with the Acceptable Use Policy (AUP) – no misuse, hacking, or unauthorized sharing.

### B. Infrastructure Provisioning

- High-speed campus-wide LAN and Wi-Fi connectivity.

- Secure VPN for remote access to resources.

- Adequate servers, storage, and cloud integration (AWS, Azure, Google Cloud).

### C. Software & Licensing

- Only licensed or open-source software permitted.

- ERP & LMS mandatory for teaching, learning, exams, and administration.

- IT Cell maintains a Software Register with license validity and renewal dates.

### D. Digital Classrooms & Learning Tools

- Smart classrooms equipped with projectors, interactive boards, lecture capture systems.

- Integration with MOOCs (SWAYAM, NPTEL, Coursera, edX).

- Online examination systems with secure proctoring tools.

### E. Data Security & Backup

- Servers protected with firewalls, IDS/IPS, endpoint security.

- Data backed up periodically with disaster recovery systems.

- Information security aligned with ISO/IEC 27001 standards.

### F. Maintenance & Upgradation

- Preventive ICT maintenance schedule (network checks, antivirus updates, software patches).

- Annual review of ICT facilities for upgrades and replacements.

- Obsolete equipment disposed of as per e-waste policy.

## 6. User Responsibilities

- Use ICT facilities only for academic and institutional purposes.

- Protect credentials (no password sharing, no unauthorized access).

- Report ICT-related issues via the ICT Helpdesk / Indent System.

- Avoid storing or transmitting objectionable/illegal content.

- Adhere to data privacy and copyright regulations.

## 7. Monitoring & Evaluation

- ICT usage tracked via ERP/LMS analytics and network logs.

- Monthly ICT Audit Reports generated (uptime, usage, issues resolved).

- ICT compliance checked during internal audits and accreditation cycles.

## 8. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- **NAAC Criterion 4:** ICT-enabled infrastructure and e-resources.

- **NBA Criterion 7:** ICT facilities for outcome-based education.

- **NIRF TLR:** ICT infrastructure supporting teaching-learning.

- **QS I·GAUGE ICT & Student Support:** Technology-enabled learning environment.

## 9. Review of Policy

- This policy will be reviewed every 3 years by the ICT Facility Committee.

- Updates will consider emerging technologies (AI, VR, cloud, IoT), digital pedagogy, and cybersecurity trends.

### ICT Facility Compliance Register

| **S. - Date of Installation / Allocation → When it was set up or allocated.

- Compliance Standard → Minimum expected requirement (e.g., 1 Gbps speed, Antivirus updated, Licensed software, AMC valid).

- Verified By → ICT Facility Committee / IQAC Coordinator / Internal Auditor.

- Remarks → Extra notes (e.g., “Pending vendor response,” “Replaced with new model”).

# 11. Infrastructure Development Policy

## 1. Introduction

- Infrastructure is a critical enabler of quality education, research, innovation, and community engagement in HEIs.

- This policy provides a structured framework for the creation, expansion, modernization, and optimal utilization of physical and digital infrastructure within the institution.

- It ensures alignment with NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE, NEP-2020, and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 9, 11, 12, 13).

## 2. Objectives

- To ensure adequate and modern facilities for teaching, learning, research, and student support.

- To adopt sustainable and eco-friendly practices in all infrastructure projects.

- To optimize space utilization through systematic planning and allocation.

- To strengthen ICT-enabled facilities for blended and digital learning.

- To create inclusive infrastructure for differently-abled persons (PwDs).

- To provide evidence-based infrastructure records for accreditation and quality audits.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- Academic spaces (classrooms, labs, libraries, seminar halls).

- Research & innovation centers (HPC labs, incubation hubs, testing facilities).

- Student facilities (hostels, canteens, sports complexes, healthcare centers).

- Administrative buildings and faculty offices.

- ICT and digital infrastructure (LMS, ERP, data centers, smart classrooms).

- Utility and support systems (power supply, UPS, water, sanitation, transport).

## 4. Principles of Infrastructure Development

- Need-Based Planning → Infrastructure projects will be based on academic/research priorities, student enrolments, and future growth.

- Master Plan Approach → Expansion aligned with long-term campus development plan.

- Sustainability → Use of green buildings, renewable energy (solar), rainwater harvesting, and waste management systems.

- Accessibility & Inclusivity → Facilities designed as per universal accessibility standards.

- Compliance with Standards → National Building Code, Fire Safety Regulations, AICTE/UGC norms.

- Technology-Integration → Smart classrooms, ICT-enabled labs, cloud integration for research.

## 5. Governance & Approvals

- **Infrastructure Development Committee (IDC):** Chaired by Dean (Planning & Development) with members from Engineering, Finance, IT, and IQAC.

- **Proposal Process:** Departments submit infrastructure requests with justifications.

- **Approval Authority:** IDC reviews and forwards proposals to Governing Body/Management Council.

- **Monitoring:** Periodic progress reports to IDC and IQAC.

## 6. Funding Sources

- Institutional budget allocations.

- Grants from UGC, AICTE, RUSA, DST, DBT, World Bank (TEQIP/EQUIP), CSR funds.

- Public-Private Partnerships (PPP).

- Alumni and philanthropic contributions.

## 7. Implementation & Monitoring

- **Project Planning:** Define scope, timelines, budget, and compliance needs.

- **Execution:** Engineering Department or outsourced contractors through transparent tendering.

- **Monitoring:** Monthly review of progress and adherence to quality standards.

- **Documentation:** Maintain records of all projects in the Infrastructure Development Register.

- **Feedback Mechanism:** Periodic user feedback from faculty, staff, and students.

## 8. Maintenance & Upgradation

- New infrastructure will be integrated into the Preventive Maintenance Policy.

- Digital systems (ERP, LMS, Smart Classrooms) will follow the ICT Maintenance Policy.

- Obsolete facilities/equipment will be phased out responsibly with proper documentation.

## 9. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- **NAAC Criterion 4:** Adequacy & modernization of infrastructure.

- **NBA Criterion 7:** Technical support & facilities for outcome-based education.

- **NIRF TLR:** Infrastructure and learning resources for teaching-learning processes.

- **QS I·GAUGE:** Facilities, governance, sustainability indicators.

- **SDGs:** Infrastructure development aligned with sustainability, accessibility, and climate action.

## 10. Review of Policy

- The Infrastructure Development Policy will be reviewed every 3 years by the Infrastructure Development Committee (IDC).

- Updates will consider new academic programs, regulatory requirements, and sustainability trends.

# 12. Space Allocation Policy

## 1. Introduction

- Physical space is one of the most valuable resources in any Higher Education Institution (HEI).

- This policy ensures that space is allocated efficiently, equitably, and sustainably across academic, administrative, research, and student-support functions.

- It aligns with institutional priorities, academic growth, accreditation standards (NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE), and national guidelines (e.g., NEP-2020).

## 2. Objectives

- To provide transparent and need-based allocation of space for teaching, research, administration, and student facilities.

- To ensure optimal utilization of available physical infrastructure.

- To promote inclusive and accessible facilities for students, faculty, staff, and differently-abled persons.

- To support sustainability goals by avoiding duplication of infrastructure and ensuring shared facilities.

- To maintain updated records of space usage for compliance, audits, and accreditation purposes.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to all:**

- **Academic Spaces:** Classrooms, lecture halls, laboratories, seminar halls.

- **Research Spaces:** Research labs, incubation centers, HPC labs, innovation hubs.

- **Administrative Spaces:** Offices for departments, examination cells, registrar offices.

- **Student Facilities:** Libraries, hostels, canteens, sports complexes, health centers.

- **Common/Shared Facilities:** Auditoriums, conference halls, IT labs, co-working spaces.

## 4. Guiding Principles

- Equity → Spaces allocated based on institutional priorities and academic needs, not hierarchy.

- Transparency → Allocation decisions made by a designated Space Allocation Committee (SAC) with documented approvals.

- Flexibility → Space reallocation permitted when requirements change (new courses, student intake increases).

- Inclusivity → Infrastructure must comply with universal accessibility standards (ramps, lifts, signage).

- Efficiency → Shared spaces (labs, auditoriums, IT facilities) encouraged to maximize utilization.

- Sustainability → Preference for multipurpose facilities and eco-friendly designs.

## 5. Governance & Responsibilities

- **Space Allocation Committee (SAC):**

<!-- -->

- **Chair:** Dean (Planning & Development).

- **Members:** Heads of Departments, Registrar, Facilities Manager, IQAC representative.

- **Roles:** Review requests, recommend allocations, monitor utilization.

<!-- -->

- **Departments:** Submit space requests with justification.

- **Facilities/Engineering Department:** Maintain updated space inventory (floor area, usage type, occupancy).

- **IQAC:** Verify compliance for audits and accreditation.

## 6. Allocation Procedure

> 1\. Request Submission → Departments submit a proposal (with details like purpose, required size, duration).
>
> 2\. Review → SAC evaluates based on need, availability, and alignment with institutional priorities.
>
> 3\. Decision → Approved requests documented with allocation order.
>
> 4\. Usage Monitoring → Periodic inspections ensure effective use and upkeep.
>
> 5\. Reallocation → Underutilized or obsolete spaces reassigned based on new requirements.

## 7. Monitoring & Utilization

- Space usage audits conducted annually by SAC.

- **Registers/logs maintained for:**

<!-- -->

- Classroom allocation & utilization.

- Lab scheduling & usage logs.

- Hostel occupancy.

- Shared facilities booking (auditorium, seminar halls).

<!-- -->

- Underutilized spaces flagged for reallocation or consolidation.

## 8. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- **NAAC Criterion 4:** Physical facilities, learning resources, and equitable access.

- **NBA Criterion 7:** Adequacy of classrooms, labs, and technical support.

- **NIRF (TLR):** Space utilization for student learning and research.

- **QS I·GAUGE (Facilities & Governance):** Transparent and inclusive infrastructure allocation.

- **SDGs (9 & 11):** Sustainable, inclusive infrastructure.

## 9. Review of Policy

- The policy will be reviewed every 3 years or earlier if required.

- Updates will reflect changes in student strength, program expansion, or regulatory norms.

# 13. Green Campus Policy

## 1. Introduction

- A “Green Campus” represents an educational environment that integrates sustainability, resource efficiency, and environmental consciousness in its physical and operational practices.

- The policy provides a structured framework for sustainable infrastructure, energy and water management, waste reduction, eco-friendly transportation, and biodiversity conservation.

- It supports institutional commitments to NAAC (Criterion 7 – Best Practices & Institutional Values), NBA (Sustainability in Facilities), NIRF (Outreach & Inclusivity), QS I·GAUGE (Environmental & Social Responsibility) and SDGs (6, 7, 11, 12, 13, 15).

## 2. Objectives

- To minimize the institution’s carbon footprint through energy, water, and waste management.

- To promote eco-friendly infrastructure development and sustainable practices.

- To create awareness among students, faculty, and staff about environmental stewardship.

- To establish the campus as a model for green and sustainable practices in higher education.

- To ensure compliance with national and international environmental standards.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- Academic, residential, administrative, and recreational buildings.

- **Outdoor facilities:** gardens, hostels, sports complexes, parking areas.

- **Utilities:** power supply, water distribution, sanitation, waste management.

- **Campus community:** students, staff, faculty, contractors, and visitors.

## 4. Key Areas of Implementation

### A. Energy Management

- Install renewable energy systems (solar, wind, biomass).

- Use LED lighting and motion-sensor-based smart lighting.

- Deploy energy-efficient appliances (Star-rated equipment, green servers).

- Conduct annual energy audits to track and reduce consumption.

### B. Water Conservation

- Install rainwater harvesting systems.

- Reuse treated wastewater for gardening and flushing.

- Promote water-efficient fixtures (aerators, dual-flush systems).

- Conduct water audits to ensure zero wastage.

### C. Waste Management

- Segregate waste at source (biodegradable, recyclable, hazardous, e-waste).

- Establish composting units for organic waste.

- Partner with certified vendors for e-waste and biomedical waste disposal.

- Adopt a paperless office system using ERP and LMS.

### D. Green Infrastructure & Landscaping

- Develop eco-friendly buildings as per Green Building Code.

- Maintain green belts, herbal gardens, and biodiversity zones.

- Encourage vertical gardens and rooftop gardens.

- Reduce use of plastic and hazardous construction material.

### E. Sustainable Transportation

- Encourage cycling and pedestrian-friendly pathways.

- Promote electric vehicle charging stations.

- Operate shared shuttle services within the campus.

- Limit fossil-fuel vehicle entry into sensitive zones.

### F. Awareness & Engagement

- Organize green campus campaigns, workshops, and competitions.

- Introduce environmental clubs and student ambassador programs.

- Integrate sustainability into the curriculum and research projects.

- Celebrate annual Green Day / Energy Conservation Week.

## 5. Governance & Monitoring

- **Green Campus Committee (GCC):**

<!-- -->

- **Chairperson:** Dean (Planning & Development) / Principal.

- **Members:** Representatives from Engineering Dept., Environment Club, IQAC, Hostel, and Student Council.

<!-- -->

- **Responsibilities:**

<!-- -->

- Approve and monitor implementation of green initiatives.

- Conduct quarterly campus audits (energy, water, waste, biodiversity).

- Submit an Annual Green Campus Report to IQAC and Governing Body.

## 6. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- NAAC → Criterion 7 (Institutional Values & Best Practices) highlights eco-friendly practices.

- NBA → Sustainability in physical facilities and technical support.

- NIRF → Outreach & Inclusivity metrics include green campus initiatives.

- QS I·GAUGE → Evaluates institutions on environmental responsibility.

- SDGs → SDG 6 (Clean Water), SDG 7 (Affordable & Clean Energy), SDG 11 (Sustainable Communities), SDG 12 (Responsible Consumption), SDG 13 (Climate Action), SDG 15 (Life on Land).

## 7. Review of Policy

- The policy will be reviewed every 3 years by the Green Campus Committee.

- Updates will include new technologies, sustainability practices, and regulatory norms.

# 14. Building Codes & Standards Compliance Policy

## 1. Introduction

- Safe, sustainable, and compliant infrastructure is critical for delivering quality education and ensuring the well-being of students, faculty, staff, and visitors.

- This policy establishes guidelines for design, construction, renovation, and maintenance of HEI buildings in alignment with:

<!-- -->

- National Building Code (NBC) of India

- AICTE/UGC Norms for academic infrastructure

- Fire Safety Regulations (National Disaster Management Authority, NFPA references)

- RPwD Act 2016 for accessibility compliance

- Environmental & Sustainability Standards (Green Building Code, IGBC/LEED)

- Local Municipal Laws & Electricity Safety Codes

## 2. Objectives

- To ensure that all buildings and facilities meet statutory, safety, and accreditation standards.

- To promote eco-friendly and sustainable construction practices.

- To integrate universal accessibility and inclusivity in all campus infrastructure.

- To create an audit-ready compliance record for NAAC, NBA, NIRF, QS I·GAUGE, and ISO certifications.

- To establish responsibilities and monitoring mechanisms for compliance.

## 3. Scope

**This policy applies to:**

- New buildings (academic blocks, labs, hostels, libraries, admin offices).

- Renovations, refurbishments, and expansions.

- Utilities and service infrastructure (water, power, sanitation, transport).

- Green and smart campus developments (solar plants, rainwater harvesting, ICT-enabled buildings).

## 4. Key Compliance Areas

### A. Structural & Safety Standards

- Compliance with NBC India (latest edition) for load-bearing, seismic resistance, wind, and material strength.

- **Fire safety systems:** alarms, extinguishers, hydrants, sprinklers, evacuation routes.

- Seismic resilience measures in earthquake-prone zones.

- Electrical installations as per Indian Electricity Rules.

### B. Accessibility & Inclusivity

- Ramps, elevators, tactile pathways, and signage as per RPwD Act 2016.

- Accessible toilets in every major block.

- Reserved parking spaces and accessible classrooms/labs.

### C. Environmental & Green Building Standards

- Energy efficiency through LED lighting, natural ventilation, and insulation.

- Green Building Certification (IGBC, LEED) for new major projects.

- Solar panels, rainwater harvesting, greywater recycling.

- Waste segregation and e-waste management.

### D. Academic & Accreditation Compliance

- Classrooms, labs, and libraries built per AICTE/UGC norms on carpet area, student-faculty ratio, and specialized equipment.

- ICT-enabled smart classrooms as per NAAC/NBA requirements.

### E. Legal & Statutory Approvals

- Construction and occupancy permissions from local municipal and fire authorities.

- Environmental clearances for large projects.

- Regular third-party audits for compliance verification.

## 5. Governance & Responsibility

- **Campus Development Committee (CDC):** Responsible for policy implementation and review.

- **Engineering/Facilities Department:** Executes construction projects and maintains compliance records.

- **Accessibility Committee:** Ensures universal design and inclusivity.

- **Green Campus Committee:** Monitors sustainability compliance.

- **IQAC:** Maintains accreditation evidence folders with compliance records.

## 6. Monitoring & Documentation

- Maintain a Building Compliance Register with project details, approvals, and certifications.

- Conduct annual safety and compliance audits.

- Include compliance checks in Monthly Campus Development Reports.

- Keep digital copies of drawings, licenses, and inspection reports for accreditation.

## 7. Accreditation & Quality Linkages

- NAAC Criterion 4 – Infrastructure adequacy, safety, and sustainability.

- NBA Criterion 7 – Facilities & technical support compliance.

- NIRF (Teaching-Learning Resources) – Infrastructure standards.

- QS I·GAUGE – Facilities, sustainability, and safety compliance.

- SDGs 9, 11, 13 – Infrastructure, sustainable cities, climate action.

## 8. Review of Policy

- This policy will be reviewed every 3 years by the CDC.

- Updates will include new regulations, emerging technologies, and best practices.
